Just a name, a category, a date. He thought about the certification office in Denvash, and the Compact clerks who'd handled him after, and every institution in between that had processed him with the gloved carefulness people used for something they weren't sure was safe to touch plainly. Greyvane did not seem interested in making him invisible, and it did not seem interested in handling him carefully either. It seemed interested in making him *scheduled* — ordinary, procedural, one more line on a board anyone could read — which was, he was beginning to understand, its own kind of visibility, and possibly the safer kind.

./books/book-04-copper-crown/chapters/chapter-13.md:202:Five notices in his life, and not one of them had ever put a number there. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-22.md:5:The first morning of it, Cael woke before Lira arrived and found himself doing something he hadn't done in weeks: reading back through the entire log from the beginning, front to back, both notebooks laid open side by side on his cot — Hesk's leather-bound one, soft now from months of handling, and his own, thicker, faster-filling, the handwriting shifting visibly across its pages from careful and deliberate in the early entries to something quicker and more confident in the recent ones. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-22.md:9:"Iron Path grants layered physical resilience," Lira explained, on the first morning of proper preparation, sketching the general shape of it in the practitioners' quarter's dirt the way Corvane had once sketched Pressure Path for him. ./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-06.md:11:The table, when it convened, was the low scarred one in the residence common room that had apparently hosted every important conversation Greyvane's students had ever needed privacy for — initials and dates burned and gouged into its edges going back decades, a stratigraphy of other people's alliances. ./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-06.md:39:"You want data," Lira said again, quieter this time, the accusation sanded down to something closer to a simple statement of fact. "We're a family."

./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-06.md:67:The middle clauses went faster, in the rhythm negotiations found once everyone had established what they were actually protecting. ./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-06.md:133:*Does it feel borrowed. I don't know. Lira says it doesn't look it. Looking and feeling aren't the same measurement, and I notice I'm reaching for hers because I can't take mine.*
./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-16.md:9:*I think I'm borrowing something. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-16.md:13:*Does Lira notice that I'm using her patterns? I don't think so. I need to be honest with myself about that distinction, because I want the first explanation to be true badly enough that I might be choosing it without enough evidence.*
./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-16.md:17:*I need more examples. One might be coincidence, even with the notice confirming it wasn't. I need a second source. Not Lira again — a different person, a different Path, watched with the same intensity, to see if the transfer happens the same way or differently or not at all.*
./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-16.md:19:This last entry sat with him longer than the others, because it implied something he hadn't fully let himself consider until he'd written it down: that if this was a general mechanism rather than something unique to Lira, then everyone he'd ever watched closely — Renn, Dessa, Sarel, Talis, the woman doing stance drills in the practitioners' quarter on his first full day in Ardenmere — might have left something behind in him, waiting, unconfirmed, for the right pressure to surface it. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-16.md:23:He chose Kestrel — a fighter he hadn't fought yet, but had watched twice from the crowd, whose Force Path had a signature he'd been quietly cataloguing without any real purpose beyond the general habit of cataloguing everything. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-07.md:73:"Circuit rating isn't Compact rating," Lira explained. "The circuit doesn't care what the Arbiter said about you. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-07.md:85:"Vell's judgment, mostly, informed by results. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-07.md:105:They talked through the morning about specifics — how bouts were structured (exchanges, not rounds; a fight ended when the Ledger-keeper called it, based on incapacitation, submission, or a fighter's own judgment that continuing wasn't worth it), how the betting economy worked (Lira had opinions about this that suggested she'd both profited and lost money from it in roughly equal measure, and a specific grudge against a bookmaker named Marrow who she claimed shaved odds in a way that wasn't technically dishonest but wasn't exactly honest either), and how a fighter built a reputation worth something.

./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:29:- [ ] **Book 6 delivery (this book — THE REVELATION; SERIES SECRET #2 DISCHARGED ON SCHEDULE):** Seln provides his intelligence cache — the files he collected during his years as a Compact operative, carried through nineteen postings in the locked case Book 4 established and Book 5 kept visible — opened on the page in Chapter 15, analyzed in Chapter 16. The files confirm: **the Guilds Compact has been falsifying Path classifications for two hundred years.** Practitioners who develop politically inconvenient abilities are reclassified into lesser Paths at their Kindling evaluation, via a modification built into the Arbiter evaluation protocol itself — a modification made so long ago that no current Compact official knows it's there. REACH discipline is absolute: the revelation is known at book's end to exactly seven people — the inner circle (Cael, Lira, Brom, Karis), Seln, Vastin (Chapter 17), and no one else. It is NOT published, NOT filed, NOT used in the proceedings, and the book argues why on the page (Chapter 19): evidence of the Arbiter protocol's corruption cannot be verified by any process that runs on Arbiters, and filing it burns Seln for nothing. The truth is carried out the door, not detonated. Its public exposure belongs to later books.

./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:144:The asset-restriction chapter — the book's coldest early dread, staged almost entirely in paperwork and letters. Seln, running the counter-scouting nobody formally asked him for (the Book 5 pattern, continued), identifies a texture change in the surveillance: the watchers are no longer watching Cael. They are watching everything *attached* to him. Records requests touch Halcenvane's sponsorship files. A registry query pings Lira's certification history, Brom's family standing, Karis's transfer file. And the beat the chapter is built around: Hesk's letter, arriving fast as ever, dry as ever, with one paragraph that stops the table — *"Two polite men from the registry came through Denvash asking after my trade licenses and the shop's title. Wanted to know if you had any interest recorded in either. I told them my grandson's interest in this shop is that he grew up in it, and they wrote that down. Whatever they're building, it has a shape. Mind the shape. — H."*
./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:146:Seln names the shape in the inner-circle council, in the flat register of a man describing his own former trade: pre-restriction inventory. Before the registry freezes an unclassified person's standing, it maps everything that standing touches — sponsors, institutions, family assets, endorsements — so the restriction lands complete on day one. It is run only when the outcome is already assumed. "They're not preparing for a ruling," he says. "They're preparing for the day after it." The chapter lets the sentence sit on the table untouched, because there is nothing to say to it — and then gives Lira the line that turns the book's second half: "So the question was never whether we win the argument. It's what we do the day after."
./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:170:The council chapter — the first honest collision over leaving, staged early and *lost* by the leavers, because the walkout must be earned and Cael is not ready. Lira puts it on the table plainly (Chapter 7's question, matured): the proceedings are a corridor; the day-after is being inventoried; the Registry Line is four days east; why are we still filing responses? The debate is the chapter's spine, four voices plus one silence, all honest. Karis argues the record: every prior institution failed them until text held it accountable; abandon the proceedings and the record reads *fled* — the system's version of a confession. Brom argues the middle: prepare both roads, walk neither yet. Cael's position is the first pole at full strength, argued with three books of receipts — the hearing, the provision, the stamps, the tournament — and one sentence that the book will spend its second half dismantling: "The system has never beaten me when the text was in the room. I'm not leaving the room while the text still works."
./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:179:Lira's chapter — the Silver-bracket debut given full featured weight, per the series' structural law that companion arcs get real page-time even while the institutional sky falls, and per this book's particular cruelty: the chapter exists to make Chapter 21 cost. Lira's first Silver-bracket meet, Rooke's program on the road, and the choreography the arc has been building since Zerin said *Silver bracket, next cycle. Be there*: she fights up a tier against a Silver Rank 8 and loses on points in the fourth exchange, extended past anything the bracket expected, and walks off the floor incandescent — because the loss is the *right kind*, the kind with six more years of doors in it, and for the first time in her life every door is open and someone is holding them. Rooke's post-bout note, four words, Rooke-length: "Again. Faster. It's there." She sleeps with the bout sheet on the table where she can see it.
./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:181:The chapter braids the counterweight through in two movements. The petition doesn't turn on evidence. It turns on discretion." The winning-doesn't-matter sentence, finally said out loud, in a room with a transcript.

./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:214:The fight is the book's first full choreography and the found family's — five practitioners plus Seln against six, on a road, for keeps. Mid-fight — and unbinds her own fixed point from inside it, stepping through a gap her Path says cannot exist. ./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:225:Two firsts, counted on the page even now, because he is who he is: the first notice to say *Silver* — the tier ladder in his log now runs Bronze, Silver, Gold, none of them his. ./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:234:The rest chapter — integration cost, aftermath, and the series' third staged birthday, deliberately the smallest and deliberately the last inside an institution's walls. ./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:236:The birthday is staged against the term's darkening sky, and the book lets both be true at once: Lira instigates, one evening, the six of them in the residence common room with the proceedings' documents deliberately stacked face-down on the far table; Brom acquires food with quiet competence; Ephram is invited — the first non-circle guest at a birthday in the series, the invitation itself a statement the chapter does not underline; Seln sits at the table like a man who has been sitting at tables all his life, and only the reader and Cael know the case is already down from its shelf. ./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:247:Seln talks for longer than he has talked in three books, in the flattest voice the book gives anyone, because the content does not need help: how it started as insurance (an operative's habit; every posting, every anomaly, duplicated and kept); how it stopped being insurance the year he noticed the anomalies had a *direction* — inconvenient abilities corrected downward, politically useful ones left alone, across postings, across decades of file-dates, across more years than any conspiracy of the living could span; how he walked away from the job in the only way his trade allows (by staying in it, and keeping); and why now — Chapter 9's silence, Chapter 11's paper, Chapter 13's road, given four sentences total. The finding, stated once, plainly, for the record the annex will never keep: **the Guilds Compact has been falsifying Path classifications for two hundred years — at Kindling, through the Arbiter protocol itself, via a modification so old that no current official knows it is there.** The room takes it the way the book has earned: Karis reaching for the memoranda with the expression she reserves for primary sources; Brom completely still; Lira's single question — "How many?" — and Seln's answer — "I stopped estimating. Estimating felt like filing." 
./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:249:And then the chapter's second movement, the debt paid: Cael answers the case in kind. ./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:251:*Close on:* the log, that night, the arc's centre of gravity: "Two hundred years. It was never a malfunction. ./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:279:What he does next is the arc, staged precisely: he does not offer alliance, does not touch the exhibits again, does not ask where the cache lives — and does not reach for a pen. He tells them, in the register of a man reading his own evaluation aloud, what he can and cannot do: he cannot verify this through any channel that would not destroy them and him to no effect — every verification instrument the Compact owns runs on the thing being verified. He will not report it — and the chapter marks that sentence as the crossing it is: an Archmarshal, in knowing possession, filing nothing; the keeper pattern, at forty years' altitude. And he will no longer lend the machine his hands: the proceedings have asked him to certify the re-evaluation protocol's applicability to the enrollee — the trap's last wanted signature, the best evaluator's name to make the corridor look like an evaluation. "They will have to proceed without my name on it. It's the only sentence I have left that they still need." Asked directly — by Lira, who else — whether he's with them, his answer keeps the bible's line exact: "No. I am not with anyone. For forty years I was an instrument, and I have just learned what I was an instrument *of*. A man should sit with that before he chooses what he becomes next." He stands, thanks the room with unperformed courtesy — the series' third staging of the gesture — and is gone.
./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:301:The stakes, paid in order. **Brom's chapter-within-the-chapter:** the advancement review's finding arrives mid-council, hand-delivered — advancement to Iron-tier, found and recommended, his family's whole ledger settled in one paragraph — with the guidance note appended in flawless registry prose: *advancement suitability weighs continued association with enrollees under active non-compliance proceedings.* Everything he ever wanted, priced at Cael. ./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:332:The walk-out — the bible's images, staged verbatim, in the order the day would actually run them, and the chapter's discipline is smallness: no speeches, no gate crowd, no institutional convulsion. ./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:354:The crossing — the road chapter, staged with the series' full weight because the series has pointed at this boundary since a fourteen-year-old was walked out of Denvash through a back exit: the east road out of Ostrand's province, four days, the country thinning — and the infrastructure thinning with it, which the chapter makes Cael catalogue because cataloguing is how he loves things: the last chartered waystation; the last posted tier-verification notice; the last watcher, who holds at the provincial boundary marker like a man at the end of a leash, logs them through his glass, and — the book's driest grace note — offers Brom a nod, professional to professional, before turning back. Holds a moment at the dark sigil — the instrument that started everything, eleven seconds of silence, seven years ago — and does not perform anything at it. NEW open threads created deliberately: the faceless faction (cold, B7+ pressure), the nameless operative (log-texture debt), Havel's transfer, Ilsev's after, Withrow's ledger, the sub-layer's base stratum (the B8 bridge), and the Compact's dawning question about what Seln kept (dread, unhurried).
./books/book-06-the-compacts-hand/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:391:- [ ] **The internal arc staged honestly at both poles:** *I can work within the system* written in the log, in confidence, with three books of receipts, Chapter 2; defended at council against Lira's honest challenge, Chapter 9; cracked on the Ostrand road, Chapter 13 ("the room was never the system"); landed intellectually in the annex, Chapter 16 ("It isn't broken. It's *maintained*."); landed finally, without triumphalism, at the fire, Chapter 24. The book's structural discipline carries the argument mechanically: the defense wins every argument (Ch8's sustained-validity, Ch10's Jent admission, Ch18's masterwork closing) and the machine proceeds anyway — the reader is never allowed to believe a better brief would have saved them, because the corridor was never an argument. And the walkout is earned by pricing, not by rhetoric: every stake is built (Ch4), lived in (Ch10, Ch19), and paid on the page (Ch21) — the book ends with the companions owning nothing the system gave them except each other and the truth, which is the Arc 2 exit the whole arc was for.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:56:Cael is 15. He has been in Ardenmere for just over a year. The book opens with a status report in his voice — dry, specific, unsentimental — as he sits in the Ironyard alcove writing in his log after a circuit bout. His circuit rating is Assessed-Copper. His observation notebook has twenty-three entries. His fragment log, recently separated from the observation notebook into its own volume, has two entries. He and Lira live in two rooms at the back of a practitioners' boarding house two streets from the Ironyard. Life is structured around the circuit.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:60:The chapter establishes the circuit as daily life rather than setting. Morning training in the side alcoves with Lira. The Ironyard in the middle hours, when practitioners drift in to watch whoever is on the main floor. The market two streets over where they buy food and where Cael has learned which vendors extend informal credit to circuit practitioners. The boarding house. The observation notebook open on the table at night.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:62:The two fragment log entries are seen: Wind-adjacent (duration: sustained) and Pressure-adjacent (duration: sustained). The Wind-adjacent entry reads: "Evasion framework, short burst. Consistent. Does not fail under pressure." The Pressure-adjacent entry is less complete — he has a working deployment range, but its upper limits under sustained combat pressure are still being characterized.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:64:*Close on:* Lira appearing in the doorway, catching him annotating the fragment log, and choosing not to ask what it is. She already has guesses. She waits until he knows.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:111:The chapter is mostly interiority, grounded in a training session with Lira. She's not exposing it to be difficult. She wants him to find it and close it, because she knows that anyone who's fought enough Wind practitioners will find it eventually. Their training is specific and useful and occasionally funny in the way that training becomes funny when two people know each other well enough to be honest about what's not working.

./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:127:**Chapter 5 — Lira's Ceiling**
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:130:Lira-focused. The circuit as her world and her problem simultaneously.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:132:Lira is rated at Copper-equivalent high range in the circuit — which means she's regularly fighting Iron-equivalent opponents in practice and winning more than she loses. Her formal classification is Copper-tier Wind Path, which is accurate to where she Kindled. The problem: Copper-tier has a ceiling, and she's at it. To advance beyond what Copper training can produce, she needs a formal Arbiter advancement evaluation — which requires guild affiliation, which requires certification clearance she doesn't have since her expulsion.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:136:This is the first time Cael asks Lira what she actually wants. Not in Ardenmere — generally. The question lands differently than she expects. She doesn't say this dramatically. She says it the way a person states something they've known for a while but rarely find worth saying aloud.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:206:Lira watches one session from over Cael's shoulder. "He's going to absorb everything you throw at him and return it." Cael: "I know." ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-17.md:5:He trained every morning with Lira, refining the Wind-adjacent step until it had lost most of its clumsiness, though it still arrived more reliably under pressure than by deliberate summons, a distinction he'd stopped trying to fully explain and simply learned to work around. Some mornings the step came clean on the first attempt, crisp and low, and he'd catch Lira nodding once in approval before resetting him for another pass. Other mornings it refused to come at all, no matter how many times he dropped into the stance that should have summoned it, and on those mornings Lira had learned not to push, just to switch the drill to something else and let the frustration burn itself out on a target that couldn't take it personally.
./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-17.md:13:He spent his afternoons rehearsing Corvane's diagram from memory — the tension that preceded a Pressure release, the difference between a fighter loading force and a fighter simply standing still — until he could close his eyes and picture the shape of it clearly enough to believe he might recognize it live, under circumstances considerably less forgiving than a dirt sketch and an unhurried afternoon. He practiced against Lira, who did her best approximation of a sustained, building pressure using her own Wind Path, though both of them understood it was an imperfect substitute — nothing in the district could truly stand in for an actual Bronze-tier professional, and the gap between simulation and reality was itself a kind of risk they simply had to accept.
./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-17.md:15:He fought twice more in the interim, smaller circuit matches that Lira insisted on scheduling specifically to keep his instincts sharp rather than let three weeks of pure preparation calcify into overthinking. One of the two opponents, a Copper Rank 3 he'd beaten in under three exchanges, asked him afterward, with genuine curiosity rather than resentment, whether it was true he had a Bronze-tier match coming. Word, apparently, had already started moving through the district ahead of the fight itself.

./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-17.md:73:Feryn built toward a release with the exact tension Corvane had taught him to watch for — the loosened shoulders, the disguised half-step, all of it arriving in the correct sequence this time, recognized rather than merely noticed. He moved. The Pressure wave that should have caught him full-on instead caught empty air where he'd stood a half-second before — close enough that he felt the edge of it pass, a wall of displaced force that stirred the dust at his feet and would have ended the fight outright if it had landed even a hand's width closer.

Contact, under the right conditions, might do it faster, or differently, or both — one data point isn't enough to say which, but it's enough to know the mechanism has more than one door into it.*

./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-05.md:53:He had spent a year and a half being observed. Even the people who loved him had, in their ways, watched him closely enough to notice when something didn't fit — Hesk cataloguing an irregular assessment, Lira clocking a pattern in a sparring match before either of them had words for it. Being watched was, if he was honest with the notebook the way he tried to always be honest with the notebook, the single most consistent experience of his entire life.

Every person since who had surprised him into trusting them — Hesk, Lira, Vell, Brom, Quenna in her cold-ledger way — had done some version of the same thing, which was to treat the verdict as unproven. ./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-05.md:131:That was the thing he kept circling, lying there with the lamp low. Not whether she was safe. Nobody was safe; safety wasn't a property people had, it was a property terms had, which was probably why Lira had said the word twice. The thing he kept circling was that for eight months, somewhere three tiers of prestige away, a person he'd never met had looked at the wreckage of his record and concluded — against her advisor, against her parents, against the entire pricing system of her world — that he was worth more, not less, for refusing to fit. He didn't know yet what to do with being valued for exactly the thing he'd been discarded for. It was going to take more than one evening.

./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-04.md:31:The binder told him what "enough" could safely mean. Four fragments, current: Wind-adjacent, Pressure-adjacent, Iron-adjacent, Compression-adjacent. Of the four, two had a shape stable enough to survive being shown to strangers who would write down what they saw. The Wind-adjacent evasion framework — a year and a half now since Lira had first taught it to him without either of them understanding what it was, drilled since through every circuit bout that had come after until it had stopped being a borrowed thing and become simply a thing he did, the way breathing was a thing he did. And the Iron-adjacent surface read — Brom's gift, quieter, a pressure-sense at the skin that told him where an opponent's weight was about to go a half-beat before it went there. Both had been shown, in cruder forms, on the Ardenmere circuit, in front of judges and crowds and at least one Compact-adjacent official. Neither was, at this point, a secret worth protecting. They were simply skill. Vell's circuit had spent the better part of a year teaching him to make that skill legible to people who were watching for exactly this — and now he was going to spend the lesson in a room with far higher stakes than a circuit bout had ever carried. ./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-04.md:67:He kept the deployment small at first — not the full circuit-bout intensity he'd used against Reydan in the last exchange of his life at Ardenmere, but a controlled, almost pedagogical version of it, moving through a sequence of evasion patterns at exactly the pace Lira had built for him: fast enough to make the framework real, slow enough that nothing about the exchange would read as combat rather than demonstration. The partner struck on Wray's count — committed, honest strikes, nothing theatrical — and Cael let the panel watch him read each one a half-beat early, adjust his stance a fraction before the strike arrived rather than after, close distance at angles that should have been impossible to anticipate and weren't. Between the second and third pass, Wray raised a finger, changed the count's rhythm without warning, and watched what happened to him when the pattern he'd been fed collapsed. What happened was the framework, doing what it did: the read came off the partner's body, not the count, and the third pass landed identical to the first two. Wray lowered the finger and wrote something. He did it four times in all, identical in structure, varied only in the angle of attack, because *consistent* was the word doing the actual work in the provision's language and he intended to satisfy it beyond any possible dispute.

./books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-03.md:107:The scheduled sessions themselves — his one sanctioned form of actual training — ran twice a week, an hour each, in whichever chalked section the roster assigned, with Quenna or a delegated assessor standing at the line with a slate. Training alone while being formally watched turned out to occupy an odd middle register between practice and performance, and it took him several sessions to stop shaping the work for the slate and start doing the work for himself, and he noted that Quenna wrote less on the days he managed it, which was either coincidence or the most useful piece of feedback she had ever declined to say out loud.

./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md:25:The compound gaze, as he'd built it, was a system for watching people who initiated. Every subject he'd studied seriously — Lira's Wind sequences, Feryn's Pressure builds, Keth's declaration chains from the audience, a year's worth of circuit fighters filed by their tells — had given him the same fundamental material to work with: the interval between intention and action. A striker decided, and the decision leaked. Weight shifted onto the driving foot a quarter-beat early. Breath caught at the top of the inhale instead of releasing through the strike. A shoulder dropped, a jaw set, a declaration's priming phase produced its small physical signature before the committed phase produced its large one. His whole notation system was organized around that leak. Three columns: *tell, interval, action.* Watch long enough and the columns filled themselves.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md:75:He laid it out on the boarding house table one evening the way he laid out any problem that resisted being held entirely in his head: the Power Log open to both entries, a loose page beside it for the working, Lira's spare whetstone weighting the corner against the draft from the window.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md:79:Wind-adjacent was the older of the two, the more thoroughly tested, the one he trusted furthest under pressure. Its function had been stable for months: an evasion framework, short-burst repositioning, a step that arrived faster than his legs alone could produce and put him somewhere an opponent's committed strike no longer reached. He knew its price the way he knew the price of bread. Each burst drew hard on his legs — a deep, immediate spending from the calves and thighs, nothing borrowed, everything paid at the moment of use — and pulled a sharp draw of breath with it, so that three bursts inside a single exchange left him breathing like a man who'd sprinted a street, and a fourth came out measurably shorter than the first. Beyond that lay the tell Lira had found and he was still training out: a small drop of the left shoulder, a half-beat before the step, that anyone who'd fought enough Wind practitioners would eventually learn to read. He'd shrunk it over a month of drills from obvious to subtle. Subtle was not gone.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md:83:Pressure-adjacent was worse, and it took him an evening of honest accounting to admit how much worse. The fragment kept two ledgers of its own — the receiving half, the slow dispersal he'd charted through the winter testing, which won nothing from an opponent who dealt no force until he chose to; and the delivery half, the one that mattered in most fights. That half's gain was force: an amplification laid into a strike at the moment of delivery, weight arriving from somewhere his mass alone couldn't have supplied. Its costs he had characterized over six patient months. It drew from somewhere deeper than the legs — the core, the breath's foundation, a spending he felt afterward as a hollowness under the ribs rather than an ache in any muscle. It demanded rhythm: deployed on-beat, integrated into a strike's natural timing, it flowed; deployed off-beat, forced into a strike that hadn't earned it, it misfired, and a misfire meant the amplification discharging partly back up his own arm — he'd sprained his wrist that way twice in the early months, and still felt the second sprain in cold weather. And it recovered slowly. Two amplified strikes in an exchange was sustainable. Three was a gamble. He had never landed four without the fourth being noticeably weaker than an unassisted hit, which defeated the entire point of the thing.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md:91:He trained with Lira through the same two weeks, mornings, and the training had the flavor of the conclusion. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-03.md:111:The session's second half, he'd asked for in advance, which was unusual enough that Lira had raised an eyebrow at the request: he wanted to work the landing beat.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-03.md:113:It was the Wind-adjacent burst's fixed price, the locked half-breath at the end of every deployment, and he'd been circling the problem for a month without touching it. The fragment gave him displacement no legs could produce — that was the purchase, and it kept being worth making. But every purchase ended the same way: weight slamming back into his heels, a half-breath where he was, in Lira's flat phrasing from the winter, *just a thing that landed,* incapable of changing direction, readable to anyone who'd learned the rhythm. Against opponents he'd out-studied, the beat never mattered; he only spent the burst when he'd already arranged for its ending to be safe. Against someone who studied him back — and his growing ledger presence guaranteed that someone eventually would — the beat was a standing invitation.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-03.md:123:The fifth through ninth attempts, he tried spending the beat early — initiating the landing before the burst fully finished, trading away the last fraction of displacement to buy back a fraction of the recovery. She caught him anyway, twice, and pulled the third strike when his balance failed outright and put him on a knee in the dirt, breath gone, hip singing its usual line of complaint from crest to knee. The fragment's costs did not scale gently with experimentation — nine bursts inside an hour was three nights' worth of ordinary use, and his body was invoicing him in real time for all of it.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-03.md:125:"That's enough," Lira said, not a question.

./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-15.md:33:It was not comfortable. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-15.md:37:He wrote the loss into the log that night at greater length than any win: *Watching isn't enough against speed that outpaces the watching. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-15.md:73:He and Lira had settled into a rhythm that felt less like instructor and student — which was, he realized somewhere around week three, not quite what it had ever technically been, since she was fifteen and self-taught in her own right and had never once claimed authority she didn't have — and more like two people solving the same problem from different angles, comparing notes, correcting each other in both directions.
./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-15.md:81:It felt absurd the first few times — announcing, mid-exchange, that his own strategy had just failed, the word coming out clipped and self-conscious while Lira grinned at him from across the yard. By the fortieth, he'd stopped needing to say it at all — the letting-go had become fast enough to happen without the word, which was, Lira told him with visible satisfaction, "the whole point of making you say it out loud in the first place. You don't keep the training wheels. You keep what the training wheels taught your legs."
./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-15.md:83:Other mornings taught him other things, less dramatically. ./books/book-01-the-shattered/chapters/chapter-15.md:119:He knew immediately what it was and where it had come from, with the same clean certainty that had told him something had changed in the first place. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-19.md:49:The drilling had a texture that his log entries, however careful, kept failing to capture. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-01.md:67:He'd added a third column to each entry this month, after six months of methodically testing what the fragments could actually do rather than simply confirming, over and over, that they existed. *Functional deployment range.* It had taken him most of the winter to work out a testing method he trusted — controlled sparring sessions with Lira, logged separately from their ordinary training, each one designed to isolate a single variable rather than let the fragments blur together as they tended to under real pressure.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-01.md:71:The third version had worked, mostly because Lira had been the one to suggest it. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-21.md:35:The burst arrived tight and fast, exactly as every account had warned, a shape in the air aimed at the center of his chest, its compression pressing against his skin before his eyes had fully tracked its path. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-21.md:51:**Second exchange.** Reydan had adjusted, and adjusted precisely — the second burst arriving at a subtly different angle than the first exchange should have prepared Cael to expect, low and canted rather than straight on, exploiting the same seam in the evasion framework that Lira had identified and closed months ago in a training-alcove session. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-21.md:65:**Third exchange.** Cael deployed his fragment suite in sequence for the first time in a real bout, under genuine combat pressure rather than the careful, controlled conditions of Brom's alcove sparring — Wind-adjacent for the evasion architecture underneath everything else, Iron-adjacent for the surface pressure-read layered on top of his own trained eye, Pressure-adjacent held ready, deliberately unused, for the moment it would actually matter rather than simply be spent. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-21.md:81:The degradation Lira's estimate had promised arrived on schedule, and knowing its schedule did nothing to slow it down. Somewhere inside that half-second of arithmetic, the last piece of the fight's logic locked into place: the win wasn't in the evasion at all. Reydan had priced this sequence assuming Cael's only currency was distance. The redirect — the purchase he'd been holding at three-for-the-fight, still unspent — bought the one thing volume couldn't defend against: proximity, paid for in damage he had, until this moment, been too solvent to need to spend.

./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-06.md:13:It wasn't a formal technique. Nobody had taught it to him — not Lira, whose own instincts ran faster and looser than his ever would, and not any circuit veteran whose brain he'd occasionally managed to pick over a meal. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-06.md:15:It had a price, which he'd itemized the same week he'd named it, because by then itemizing prices had become policy. The compound gaze was expensive in exactly the currency his method ran on: attention. Run at full depth, it consumed him — two exchanges of true phase-reading and his eyes ached as though he'd read a full evening in bad lamplight; a whole bout at depth left the ache sunk behind his forehead for hours. Worse, it narrowed him. Watching one fighter's grammar meant not watching the room; the periphery he ordinarily kept — the low-grade awareness of who sat where and who moved when, a habit the circuit had trained into him for free — dimmed to almost nothing while the gaze was engaged. He'd measured the narrowing once, deliberately, having Lira move through his periphery while he read a bout at depth, and the result had unsettled him into writing it in ink: *at full depth I am functionally blind past ten degrees. The method's cost is the method's condition. Do not run the gaze anywhere I can't afford to be blind.* So he rationed it: standard watching for the first exchanges, the gaze deployed in deliberate pulls of an exchange at a time, saved for the moments when structure mattered more than surroundings.

./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-10.md:5:Her opponent was an Iron-equivalent Wind Path practitioner named Wendel — formally trained, senior to Lira in every credential that could be written down, the kind of fighter whose technique had the settled polish of someone who'd never once had to teach himself anything the hard way. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-10.md:7:He watched this bout with the notebook open, out of habit, but the watching itself had changed since the last time he'd charted Wendel. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-10.md:11:Wendel absorbed the opening with visible surprise — not alarm, just the recalibration of someone who'd expected a more cautious Copper-equivalent opponent and was rapidly revising the assessment. ./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-10.md:13:She didn't think in single exchanges anymore. Not damage-free; she'd carry a mark from it by evening. But not the fight-ending blow it had been built to be. She used the opening it cost her to close distance rather than retreat, which was, Cael understood watching it happen, exactly the kind of decision the old Lira — the clean-win Lira — would never have made. Retreating was safer. Closing was better.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-10.md:15:The middle exchanges ran long, longer than most of her recent bouts, both fighters trading real ground rather than either one settling into a controlling rhythm. Hers arrived inside the exchange itself. Wendel's whole difficulty could be read in his reset stance, which came a fraction later each time, the filing-away gesture growing hurried as the fights he was filing stopped resembling the ones he'd trained for.

./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:1:# Chapter 5 — Lira's Ceiling
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:3:Lira's circuit rating had settled, over the past several months, into a shape that told an increasingly frustrating story: Copper-equivalent, high range, regularly fighting Iron-equivalent opponents in practice and winning more often than she lost.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:7:He had a baseline for her, which was the thing that made the watching legible. A year of mornings had given him the most complete observational record he kept on anyone — not written down, most of it, because writing down a friend felt different from writing down an opponent, but held the same way: her resting rhythm between combinations, the exact economy of her resets, the tell she didn't know she had when a drill stopped teaching her and became stubbornness instead (her jaw set a fraction forward; her reset time shortened when it should have lengthened). Watching a stranger, he had to build the picture from nothing, four bouts and three columns. Watching Lira, he only had to watch the deviations — and the deviations, lately, had all pointed the same direction. Longer sessions. Shorter rests. The paid drills with her Bronze-tier washout partner running past their agreed hour, which cost her extra and which she paid without mentioning.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:9:The alcove was quiet by the time he found her that evening, the Ironyard's main floor already handed over to the night's last scheduled bout, its noise a distant murmur through the warehouse's old walls. Someone had left a lamp burning low near the practice post, casting Lira's shadow long and doubled across the packed dirt, stretching and contracting each time she reset her stance. She was running the same combination over and over, alone now — her formal training partner, a Bronze-tier washout who sparred with her twice a week for a modest fee, had already packed up and gone home to whatever life he kept outside these walls.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:37:Lira sat down on the bench beside him, the drill apparently abandoned for the moment. "Combat performance is most of assessed rating, for people who have one. But formal advancement — the kind that moves you from Copper to Iron on the registry, not just in some circuit's private ledger — requires the Arbiter to actually evaluate you again. A second Kindling-style event, essentially, except it only happens if a guild sponsors the request. No guild, no sponsor. No sponsor, no evaluation. I could fight flawlessly for the next ten years and my formal classification would still read Copper-tier Wind Path, because nobody with the standing to request otherwise has any reason to bother."
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:55:Lira went quiet in a way that was different from her usual quiet — not gathering a response, but genuinely weighing whether to give the real one or a smaller, safer version of it.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:67:Cael didn't try to solve it. He'd learned, over a year of watching her work through problems, that Lira rarely wanted a solution handed to her — she wanted the actual shape of the problem named clearly enough that she could work it herself, the same instinct that had made him useful to opponents' patterns for a year now, turned, for once, toward someone he genuinely loved rather than merely studied.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:99:By Lira's old standard, the bout would have gone one of two ways: a careful, patient win built on picking Dravin apart from a safe distance, or a controlled loss that cost her nothing but pride. She chose neither. She pressed from the opening exchange, closing distance faster than the situation strictly called for, taking a glancing hit along her shoulder in the second exchange that she could have avoided entirely with the old, cautious footwork she'd spent months perfecting.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:103:Closed, but not idle. He was running the bout through his method anyway, because the method no longer required the pen — and watching Lira against a formally trained Wind practitioner was the richest comparative data the circuit had offered him in months. Dravin fought the technique as written: every evasion initiated from the same textbook load, every declaration arriving inside the same tempo his training had installed, a man performing an excellent copy of his instruction. Lira fought the technique as discovered — and the difference showed in the deviations. Dravin never surprised himself. Lira, pressing lines she'd always declined, surprised herself twice in the first three exchanges, and Cael could see her *harvesting* the surprises in real time, each one visibly banked mid-fight, the exact behavior he performed on a notebook page executed instead at full speed inside an exchange. It was costing her. That was the other thing he could see, and it sat in his chest with a weight that watching strangers never produced: every piece of information she bought, she bought with her own body, and he had to hold himself still on the bench each time the purchase went through.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:119:The bout after that went better, and Cael watched this one with the same close attention he'd have given a genuinely difficult opponent of his own, because something about the way Lira fought when she wasn't holding anything back had started to teach him things even his own methodical study of strangers rarely produced.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:121:Her opponent that day was faster than Dravin had been, a younger Iron-equivalent competitor with a reputation for controlling the pace of a fight from the opening exchange onward. He tried to do exactly that. Lira let him, for one exchange — conceding the rhythm deliberately, the way Cael himself had once conceded a fight's early tempo to Dessa, watching rather than fighting — and then, in the second exchange, did something Cael hadn't seen from her before: she matched his pace exactly, beat for beat, close enough that the crowd's murmur shifted audibly, the unmistakable sound of an audience realizing a fight had stopped being predictable.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:133:Vell noticed too, the way Vell noticed most things that mattered to the ledger's honesty. She found Cael one afternoon, midway through the stretch, watching Lira spar from the alcove doorway.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:177:Lira considered the question with real seriousness, turning it over the way she turned over a genuinely difficult opponent's tell. "Both, maybe. I don't spend time imagining the actual conversation — what I'd say, what they'd say back. That part doesn't interest me much. What interests me is just — existing, loudly enough, that the decision they made stops looking correct to anyone who examines it. I don't need them in the room for that to happen. I just need to become undeniable enough that the story reaches them eventually, secondhand, the way most consequences travel."
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:185:"I think you're going to get there," he said, plainly, because it was true and because he'd learned, over a year, that Lira trusted plain statements more than elaborate encouragement.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-05.md:197:*Lira learned to lose usefully. Watching her do it is teaching me something about my own method that watching opponents never has — that the information was always available at this cost, and I've been paying a version of it too, for a year, without fully naming what I was buying with it.*
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:27:Lira found him during the gap between the second and third preliminary bouts, settling onto the bench beside him without asking, the two of them watching the crowd's texture shift as more people filtered in through the doors.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:61:Cael filed all of it without visible reaction, the way he filed most information that mattered too much for an immediate response. He didn't mention it to Lira or Brom yet, not that evening. He wanted to understand the shape of it fully — why an academy would send a scout to an unsanctioned circuit at all, and why now, on the eve of a fight that had already drawn more attention than anything the Ironyard had hosted in a year — before deciding what, if anything, the information actually required him to do.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:67:He wrote a short entry into the observation notebook before he went to find Brom and Lira, the private section rather than the tactical one, because it felt closer to a question than a fact: *Academy scout. Watching, not participating. Doesn't want to be watched back, or doesn't care to be seen wanting that. Tomorrow is either going to be a fight, or it's going to be a fight that becomes something else entirely by the time it's over. I don't know which yet.*
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:81:"Lira thinks you're ready," Brom said. "I think you're ready. I don't think either of those things matter as much as whether you think it, though, so I'm asking you directly instead of just telling you what we've already decided." A pause, uncharacteristically long for him. "Do you think you're ready?"
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:85:"Good." Something in Brom's shoulders eased, very slightly — not relief exactly, more like the settling of a question he'd genuinely needed answered before he could let the night close. "Then go be alone with it for a while. I'll walk Lira back. We'll see you in the morning."
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:93:Three integrated fragments. One anomaly that might, eventually, become a fourth, if the anomaly ever consented to be summoned on purpose rather than simply happening to him under pressure he couldn't fully control. He turned the pages back through the log's short history, and further back in memory than the log itself reached — Renn's telegraphed strikes, his room at Torvin's boarding house by lamplight, Dessa's transition windows, Feryn's contact-sourced fragment, Brom's careful, patient teaching, the Tide-adjacent anomaly he still couldn't name. A year of careful, accumulated evidence, building toward a night like this one without either of them ever quite planning for it to arrive this way.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:95:He took the body's inventory first, because tomorrow's plan would be spent from the body's account. Two weeks of Lira's drilling had left him lighter and faster than he'd ever been, and marked in ways the log recorded without commentary: the deep bruising along both forearms mostly yellowed out, the ribs quiet unless pressed directly, the ankles — his one real concern nine days ago — settled back into reliability. She'd tapered the final three days deliberately, cutting intensity while keeping rhythm, so that his legs arrived at tonight rested without going soft. He could feel the difference even sitting still, a coiled readiness underneath the fatigue: a body that had been asked for more than it had ever given, and had, somewhere in the second week, agreed to provide it.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:99:*Wind-adjacent: effectively unlimited at ordinary deployment rates — but "ordinary" assumes ordinary tempo. Against sustained burst pacing, the cost compounds. Lira's estimate, and mine: continuous full-speed evasion holds for three exchanges, degrades in the fourth. Plan around the degradation, not around hope.*
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-20.md:111:He didn't go find Lira and Brom right away. He sat a while longer instead, alone with the last of the evening's quiet, letting the weight of tomorrow settle into something he could actually carry rather than simply brace against — the hard-won steadiness of someone who had learned, the slow way, that the waiting before a fight that mattered was survivable if you let yourself feel the whole size of it first, rather than rushing past the feeling toward the fight itself.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-24.md:18:This was the fragment whose incomplete version had first appeared, instinctively, in the Reydan bout. It was confirmed now, given a partial name, integrated.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-24.md:22:He turned to the anomaly pages first, before writing anything new. Two entries had sat there since the bout: session nine, and the half-second. He drew a line under the second and marked it resolved — *identified: Compression-adjacent, incomplete expression preceding integration* — and felt the small, disproportionate satisfaction of a column finally balancing. Then he looked for a long moment at the first entry, the session-nine reading Brom had called Tide-adjacent, which the new notice did nothing at all to explain. Still unreproduced. Still unexplained. One anomaly remained one anomaly. He left the page exactly as it was.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-24.md:60:"I said goodbye to the people worth saying it to. You. Lira. Dace, this morning, since he was standing right there." Brom shrugged, unbothered. "I don't collect farewells from people who were never going to remember me past the season anyway. That's not coldness. That's just accuracy."
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-24.md:84:Lira, walking beside him, read the note over his shoulder without asking permission — an old habit between them by now, worn smooth from a year of sharing everything worth sharing. "That's the whole district in one sentence," she said. "Nobody here cared what you were classified as. They cared whether you fought honestly. That's the entire value system, and I don't think either of us fully appreciated how rare it is until right now, walking away from it."
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-24.md:106:The road east was early and quiet, Ardenmere's district gate receding behind them, Greyvane several days ahead. Cael, Lira, Brom. The pace was easy. No rush.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-24.md:110:"You're doing the thing," Lira said, watching him watch the horizon.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-24.md:142:"It's more than we had when we got here," Lira added.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:5:Sunday mornings had a texture in Ardenmere that Cael had come to value without ever quite deciding to — the market two streets over didn't open until later, the Ironyard's side alcoves stayed empty until closer to midday, and the boarding house itself settled into the kind of hush that let a person hear their own thinking clearly for once. The building's sounds simplified down to their skeleton: the landlady's kettle at first light, one floor down and two rooms over; the practitioner in the corner room running through her breathing exercises, a slow four-count he could set his own pulse by; Lira's door staying shut a full hour longer than any other day of the week, the single luxury she permitted herself. He'd taken to using the hour for exactly this sort of work: the unglamorous, structural labor of organizing what he knew so that it would still be useful to him in six months, rather than just true today and forgotten by winter.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:17:He hadn't needed to buy anything for today's work — the slim volume was still, months later, the smallest-bound book the stationer near the market carried, and it had pages enough left for what he actually needed to do. He knew that stationer's stock as well as he knew Vell's shelf by now: three grades of paper, of which only the middle one took ink without feathering; bindings from a workshop in the Ranked core, sold here at a district markup the stationer never apologized for; ink at four coppers the bottle, which meant the year's writing — both books, all of it — had cost him less than a single bout's purse. He'd done that arithmetic once, idly, and been quietly satisfied by it. Everything he'd learned this year, everything the notebooks held, had been purchased for four bottles of ink and the willingness to actually look. The looking was the expensive part. The looking cost him something no vendor priced. What it needed wasn't replacing. It needed reorganizing: the deployment-range column he'd finished for Fragment 1 sat two pages away from Fragment 2's conditions, separated by an unrelated aside about Lira's technique that had wandered in months ago and never left. He wanted the whole book restructured — one consistent format, front to back, so that in six months he could open to any entry and know exactly what he'd known when he wrote it, without having to reconstruct the shape of his own thinking from scattered marginalia.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:24:*Source: sustained observation, Lira, roughly three months of morning training preceding first confirmed acquisition.*
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:41:Reading the two entries side by side in the new format also showed him something the scattered version had hidden: the fragments had opposite temperaments. The Wind fragment was fast, cheap per use, reactive, and got *more* reliable under pressure; the Pressure fragment was slow, expensive, deliberate, and had never once fired without his explicit intention. One behaved like a reflex he'd been lent. The other behaved like a tool he'd been handed. Whether that difference traced to their sources — months of watching Lira versus one second of Feryn's contact — or to something about the abilities themselves, he had no way to test with a sample of two. He added it to the open questions anyway. A sample of two was still a pattern worth writing down as a pattern-shaped guess, clearly labeled as one.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:47:Lira found him an hour later than planned, notebook still open, and didn't ask what had kept him — she'd learned, over the year, exactly which silences meant *give me a minute* and which meant *I'm avoiding something,* and this one was clearly the first kind.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:59:She said it lightly, but Cael had learned, over a year of mornings exactly like this one, that Lira's version of affection rarely announced itself as affection directly. It arrived instead as a challenge, an insult delivered with a grin, an offer to spar dressed up as an accusation that he'd gotten soft — and somewhere underneath all of it, unstated but entirely reliable, the simple fact that she'd shown up again this morning, the way she'd shown up every morning for a year, because some part of her had decided a long time ago that this was worth doing every single day rather than most of them.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:63:They trained through the morning with the rhythm they'd built over a year of mornings — drills, sparring, the constant correction that came from two people who'd stopped needing to soften feedback for each other months ago. Lira was working a particular angle today, deliberate about it in a way Cael recognized from experience: she'd found something, and she was building toward telling him about it rather than simply announcing it outright, the way she preferred to make a point land through demonstration rather than declaration whenever the lesson allowed for it.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:79:Finding it took him most of the next half hour, and the finding was its own education, because the seam turned out to live somewhere he'd never thought to look: not in the fragment, but in himself. The Wind-adjacent burst began, every time, with the hip-drop — and the hip-drop began, every time, on his left. He'd never chosen that. His body had simply imported the movement whole from wherever it had imported it, left-initiated, and a burst that always initiated left broke, always, toward the same family of angles: forward and left, back and left, a fan of escape routes that covered perhaps two-thirds of the circle around him. The remaining third — his right-rear quarter — wasn't undefended, exactly. His ordinary footwork covered it, at ordinary speed. But the fragment didn't go there, had never gone there, and an opponent who herded him until the only escape lay through the right-rear quarter would be fighting a boy with no fragment at all. That was what Lira had been doing, twice, in twenty minutes: not beating the burst, but steering it into the one direction it refused to fire.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:85:Closing it was not a matter of deciding to. He spent the next stretch of the session trying to initiate the drop from his right hip, and the attempts were humbling in an instructive way — the fragment simply declined. The left-hip drop summoned that familiar loosening, the floor's borrowed half-inch, the hollow-boned launch; the right-hip drop summoned nothing but an ordinary, mortal sidestep executed by an ordinary, mortal boy who'd just telegraphed his intention by dropping his hip for no reason. It was like discovering that a word he used daily could only be spoken in one direction. The fragment wasn't his, not fully — the entry in the Power Log said *integration: partial* and here, in his hips, was what partial meant, rendered physical: the movement had come from watching Lira, and Lira, he realized with something between amusement and vertigo, initiated her own Wind evasions off her left hip. He had inherited her asymmetry along with her technique. He was carrying her fingerprint in his skeleton.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:89:Lira stopped mid-reset. "Mine does what?"
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:95:That was the work of the rest of the morning: not changing the fragment, but rebuilding the frame around it. Lira drilled him on angle-denial — reading the herding pattern three steps early, spending ordinary footwork cheaply and continuously to keep his left-forward fan open, treating the right-rear quarter as ground to be defended by position rather than by escape. It was unglamorous, exhausting, entirely fragmentless work, and by the end of it the seam had closed the only way it could close: not sealed, but guarded. She tried the herding angle twice more, adjusting each time, and both attempts died three steps early, before the trap's geometry ever finished assembling.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:111:Lira stopped too, immediately, reading the stillness for what it was. She'd seen this before, though not often enough to have stopped finding it remarkable.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:125:"What happened?" Lira asked, quietly, giving him the space to answer or not.
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:137:Lira considered this with the seriousness she brought to any genuinely new information. "So it's not you getting better at reading it. It's the thing itself doing something."
./books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-04.md:155:*Also today, from the seam work: the fragment is left-handed because Lira is left-handed. I am carrying her instructor's habits, two people removed. Whatever the fragments are, they are not abstractions. They are copies of real people, taken on real days, asymmetries included. Worth remembering that everything I ever borrow will come with fingerprints.*

books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-108-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-109-He writes the first systematic Power Log entry. The two confirmed fragments are each given a structured record: what it is (as best he can determine), when he acquired it, under what conditions, what its current deployment range is, and what he doesn't yet know. The Wind-adjacent entry is the most complete — he's had it longest and tested it most. The Pressure-adjacent entry is the less complete of the two — he has a working deployment range but its upper limits under sustained combat pressure remain untested.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-110-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:111:The chapter is mostly interiority, grounded in a training session with Lira. She's pushing him in a specific direction today: she has identified that his evasion framework (Wind-adjacent deployment) has a seam — a gap in the coverage angle that she can exploit with her own Wind techniques. She's not exposing it to be difficult. She wants him to find it and close it, because she knows that anyone who's fought enough Wind practitioners will find it eventually. Their training is specific and useful and occasionally funny in the way that training becomes funny when two people know each other well enough to be honest about what's not working.

books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-129-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-130-Lira-focused. The circuit as her world and her problem simultaneously.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-131-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:132:Lira is rated at Copper-equivalent high range in the circuit — which means she's regularly fighting Iron-equivalent opponents in practice and winning more than she loses. Her formal classification is Copper-tier Wind Path, which is accurate to where she Kindled. The problem: Copper-tier has a ceiling, and she's at it. To advance beyond what Copper training can produce, she needs a formal Arbiter advancement evaluation — which requires guild affiliation, which requires certification clearance she doesn't have since her expulsion.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-133-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-134-She's been working around this by studying the technique architecture of Iron-tier Wind Path practitioners from the outside — watching their movements in bout, deriving the declarations she can't formally access from the physical evidence they produce. It's partly working. It's not enough. She knows the gap between what she's doing and what an actually-advanced Copper-tier or Iron-tier Wind practitioner can do, and she knows the gap is growing rather than closing, because she's improving but her Path's formal tier isn't. She can't access what the Arbiter hasn't given her. She can approach it from outside. She cannot arrive.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-135-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:136:This is the first time Cael asks Lira what she actually wants. Not in Ardenmere — generally. The question lands differently than she expects. She takes a real pause before answering. "I want to be the best Wind practitioner alive. I want it to be obvious. I want someone in the guild to have to acknowledge it even though they threw me out." She doesn't say this dramatically. She says it the way a person states something they've known for a while but rarely find worth saying aloud.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-137-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-138-Cael doesn't try to solve it. He says: "You're going to need Iron-equivalent opponents who are actually trying." She: "I know." He: "Then stop holding back against the ones you fight." She stares at him. "I've been holding back." It isn't a question. He: "You've been winning cleanly instead of learning. They're not the same thing." She sits with this. Then: "Alright." She stops holding back starting with her next bout. It changes her circuit trajectory — she starts winning messily and extracting more information from each fight.

He's dangerous not just because of power but because of intelligence.

The inward compression is a tell, if you can read it." Cael: "I have a partial pressure-read." Brom: "I know. Is it functional?" Cael: "It's developing." Brom: "Then develop it. You have two weeks."

books/book-03-no-path-given/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-183-
books/book-03-no-path-given/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-184-Karis works from observables only (per the terms — she has never seen the Power Log and doesn't ask). Her data: Cael's capabilities cluster around the signatures of people he has spent sustained time observing at close range. Her stated hypothesis, delivered to Cael and Lira and Brom with academic formality in the common room: "Whatever your architecture does, observation is upstream of it. Every capability I can document corresponds to prolonged exposure to a practitioner. If exposure is the input, the process should — in principle — be *directable*." The room goes still. She has, from the outside, with no access to the log, reconstructed the outline of the thing the three of them have been circling for two books. Brom, softly: "Told you she had a method."
books/book-03-no-path-given/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-185-
books/book-03-no-path-given/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md:186:Cael's internal movement is the chapter's real content. Directing it has never occurred to him as a possibility — the fragments *arrive*, they have always arrived, on their own schedule, like weather. The idea that the weather might have a handle on it is destabilizing precisely because of what it implies: if it can be aimed, then what happened with Lira's Wind, with Feryn, with Brom, with Reydan, was never random. And if it was never random, he is not a person things happen to. He is a person who does things — and has been, without consent, to people he cares about. He takes this to Lira alone, at night, the way he took the first disclosure to her in Book 1. "I might have been taking from you on purpose without knowing it was on purpose." Lira, steady, after a long moment: "You told me what you knew when you knew it. Keep doing that, and there's nothing to forgive." It is the Book 1 Ch16 covenant, renewed under heavier load.
books/book-03-no-path-given/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-187-
books/book-03-no-path-given/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md-188-*Close on:* the Power Log — the entry that titles the internal arc: "If it can be directed, I have to learn to direct it. Not to have more. To stop not-knowing what I'm doing. Not-knowing is the version that eventually gets someone killed."

books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-08.md-58-
books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-08.md-59-Glass Path ran on discrete commitments. Each declaration was a crystalline structure thrown whole into the space between two practitioners — sharp-edged, precise, unable to be revised once released the way Wind Path's evasions could be feathered mid-motion or Iron Skin's bracing could be adjusted a half-beat late. A Glass Path practitioner did not improvise. He selected, in advance, and executed, and the execution either landed clean or it didn't, and either way the structure was already spent by the time anyone could react to it. The whole discipline organized itself around one problem: minimizing the gap between one commitment ending and the next beginning. It was, Cael wrote on the second night, an unforgiving Path to train seriously, and the rigor of it explained a great deal about the person training it. Edran did not waste motion, because a Glass Path practitioner who wasted motion in drilling wasted motion in the one place where waste was least survivable — and seven years of never being permitted an inefficient rep explained, better than any rumor about him did, how he'd risen to Iron-tier Rank 2 as Greyvane's top enrolled student before Karis had ever transferred in.
books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-08.md-60-
books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-08.md:61:It took four more days to find the seam, and he found it the way he'd found Keth's, and Darrow Innes's, and Reydan's before it — not by watching for a mistake, because Edran made almost none, but by watching for the place where precision itself created a cost. Between the second and third declarations in any sustained sequence, there was a recommitment window — a fraction of a second where the second crystalline structure released and the third had not yet formed, a gap built into the discipline's own architecture rather than into Edran's execution of it. Every Glass Path practitioner would carry some version of that seam, Cael suspected, the same way every Wind Path practitioner carried Lira's evasion-reset vulnerability. Discipline created structure. Structure created seams. He wrote the pattern down plainly: *The gap isn't his mistake. It's the shape of the thing itself. Patience, not correction.*
books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-08.md-62-
books/book-03-no-path-given/chapters/chapter-08.md-63-He spent the fifth and sixth days confirming it from three different angles — watching Edran drill alone in the early mornings, watching him spar against two different training partners in the afternoons, watching the recording of a bout Wray had on file from before Karis had ever transferred in — and the gap held every time, unvarying, structural rather than incidental. He did not train visibly against it. A seam anyone could see coming stopped being useful the moment it was seen, and he had learned that lesson expensively enough, against enough opponents, to trust it without needing to relearn it here.

books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-24-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md:25:The compound gaze, as he'd built it, was a system for watching people who initiated. Every subject he'd studied seriously — Lira's Wind sequences, Feryn's Pressure builds, Keth's declaration chains from the audience, a year's worth of circuit fighters filed by their tells — had given him the same fundamental material to work with: the interval between intention and action. A striker decided, and the decision leaked. Weight shifted onto the driving foot a quarter-beat early. Breath caught at the top of the inhale instead of releasing through the strike. A shoulder dropped, a jaw set, a declaration's priming phase produced its small physical signature before the committed phase produced its large one. His whole notation system was organized around that leak. Three columns: *tell, interval, action.* Watch long enough and the columns filled themselves.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-26-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-27-Brom initiated almost nothing. He stood, and things happened to him, and he answered them. The columns stayed empty.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-28-

books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-76-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-77-Two fragments. Against Brom, both of them ran into trouble.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-78-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md:79:Wind-adjacent was the older of the two, the more thoroughly tested, the one he trusted furthest under pressure. Its function had been stable for months: an evasion framework, short-burst repositioning, a step that arrived faster than his legs alone could produce and put him somewhere an opponent's committed strike no longer reached. He knew its price the way he knew the price of bread. Each burst drew hard on his legs — a deep, immediate spending from the calves and thighs, nothing borrowed, everything paid at the moment of use — and pulled a sharp draw of breath with it, so that three bursts inside a single exchange left him breathing like a man who'd sprinted a street, and a fourth came out measurably shorter than the first. Beyond that lay the tell Lira had found and he was still training out: a small drop of the left shoulder, a half-beat before the step, that anyone who'd fought enough Wind practitioners would eventually learn to read. He'd shrunk it over a month of drills from obvious to subtle. Subtle was not gone.
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-80-
books/book-02-iron-circuit/chapters/chapter-09.md-81-Against most opponents, the ledger balanced easily: the cost of the burst was almost always smaller than the cost of being hit. Against Brom, the arithmetic collapsed, because Brom did not chase. An evasion framework earned its keep by making committed attacks miss, and Brom committed to almost nothing. Cael could spend his legs dancing around a man who was content to stand on his heels and wait, and at the end of it Brom would have paid nothing and Cael would have paid for every step. Wind-adjacent, against this opponent, wasn't a weapon. It was, at best, insurance — worth carrying, ruinous to rely on.
